# stations E16: sunrise and sunset around explicit operations (lane contract)

Step E16 of the skills v2 execution plan (Part E, E16; plan amendment A8; decision D6; plan ruling
17). Drafted by the control room on 2026-10-11, after E15 closed and merged (`main` at `5cf37d5`,
pull requests 86 and 87) and while the E15 punch-list round (E15 contract, A34) was building.
**Status: the picks are RULED (section 18, A1, 2026-10-11); no slice has the owner's go.** Section 5
holds what the owner ruled before this document existed; section 4 holds the picks as they were put
to him, one at a time in the control room's window. No builder starts on this document before the
punch list has merged, the frame document (`2026-10-12-stations-e16-frame.md`, the single source
for the frame; A5, A6) has passed the outside reviewer's read, each lane's own rows have passed
theirs before that lane's builder starts, and the owner has typed the go for slice 1.

The E15 lane contract (`docs/plans/2026-10-01-stations-e15.md`, amendments A1 to A34) stays the
authority for `vertical-v2`, `handoff-v2` and `ship-v2`; the E14 contract for the four front cores
and their shared frame; the E13 contract for `build-v2`, `signoff-v2` and `recheck-v2`; the E12
contract for the records component. Nothing in this step changes what any of those decides.

Contents: 1 What E16 delivers · 2 Evidence · 3 Boundaries · 4 Owner picks · 5 Owner rulings
(given) · 6 Rulings of this contract · 7 Slice 1: the lifecycle frame · 8 Sunrise, the local half ·
9 The providers · 10 Sunset and restore · 11 Slice 5: the join · 12 Disposable-project checks ·
13 Slices, crew, worktrees · 14 Review and close · 15 Reporting · 16 What is still open · 17 What this step never does · 18 Amendments.

## 1. What E16 delivers

Plan text, verbatim. **E16. Rebuild sunrise and sunset around explicit operations.** **Artifact:**
Shared lifecycle code, provider integrations, preview manifests, templates, and restoration
instructions. **Done when:** Disposable-project checks demonstrate correct vault/machine
preflight, staged-document adoption, credential-safe execution, declared Git behavior, resumable
partial failure, and restoration. Sunset's default preserves records without depending on a Trash
expiration window. **Runs:** X + S; C handles Claude-owned integration checks; O handles actions
requiring account/UI authority; R reviews. **Dependencies:** E12, E15. **Owner gate:** D6 for any
broader bootstrap Git exception. Real service operations require the authorization named in their
preview.

The plan, beside it: "The sound `.env.example` checks and database backup requirements remain. The
script extraction must test tracing and error output, not merely move the commands."

Plan amendment A8: "The lifecycle scripts follow the A7a helper interface, and the script
extraction tests the tracing and error-output paths for credential exposure. D6 as ruled: sunrise
may push the initial scaffold to `main` of a brand-new repo." Decision register 13 (D6), the owner:
"Sunrise push to main is fine because it'll be spinning up a brand new repo and everything, and
pushing to main won't hurt anything."

**Runs, read under the owner's crew ruling of 2026-10-11 (section 5, R2).** His ruling replaces
the plan's "X + S" line for this step: one Opus 5.5 builder at high per slice; the outside reviewer
(Astra, GPT-6, through `codex exec` on a sealed copy) at low is the checker of every hand-back; a
script with no model runs the suites. "C handles Claude-owned integration checks" is read as the
Claude adapter's own checks (section 6, E16-14). "O handles actions requiring account/UI authority"
is read as the owner: an operation only his account or his click can perform is his, shown to him
with the exact step and never attempted by the tool (E16-4; when it counts as done is the frame's
OP-9).

In plain words: sunrise starts a project on every layer it needs and sunset retires one on every
layer it lives on. v1 does both as prose a model follows, 541 and 316 lines. E16 rebuilds them so
that every change to the machine or to an outside service is an **operation**, as the frame
defines one (`2026-10-12-stations-e16-frame.md`, OP-1, OP-2, ROW-1). Five things get built.

1. **The lifecycle frame (slice 1).** The operation record, the plan of operations (the manifest),
   the preview rendered from it, the run journal, resume, the credential guard, the provider
   interface with fake providers, and the vault and machine gate.
2. **Sunrise, the local half (slice 2).** The new repo and its doc kit, staged-document adoption,
   the vault entry, memory and the terminal shortcut, as operations.
3. **The providers (slice 3).** GitHub, Vercel, Supabase through the Vercel Marketplace, and the
   provider-blind database backup, each behind the frame's interface, each with a fake.
4. **Sunset and restore (slice 4).** Retirement as operations whose default leans on no expiry
   window, and a restore that is a command (P16, as reworded by the owner's word in A6; where a
   restore gets its plan is the frame's EV-5 and EV-7).
5. **The join (slice 5).** One throwaway project taken through sunrise, sunset and restore on the
   real services with the owner present (P12), install proofs on both harnesses, marketplace
   entries, README counts, guide findings, the full outside review.

Out of scope: Notion on any layer (R3); any provider outside R4; cutover or retirement of the v1
`sun` plugin (P14; E17 to E19); any change to `plugins/records/`, `plugins/readers/` or the ten v2
cores (E16-2); the carried E15 punch-list items (R6, E18's pre-release round); the OpenCode
adapters; a third harness.

**Measured in v1 on `5cf37d5`, for the picks.** Notion is named on 26 lines of sunrise and 15 of
sunset. Every one of the 7 lines of sunset that names the Trash names Notion's (the board is
"moved to trash, restorable ~30 days"); with Notion out, no v1 step leans on a Trash window, and
what remains of that class is each provider's own retention clock on a paused resource (section
16; the catalog's Part 6, M3). Neon is named on 4 lines of each skill. "npm publish" is a column of sunrise's
project-type table and no phase publishes anything. v1 passes the database connection string to
`pg_dump` as an argument and the Vercel token to `curl` in a header argument: both are visible in
the process table while the command runs (the frame's SEC-2 and SEC-3 close both).

## 2. Evidence every slice reads

Read completely before starting:

1. This document, the frame document (`2026-10-12-stations-e16-frame.md`) in full, the operation
   catalog (`2026-10-12-stations-e16-operations.md`; its Part 4 is a draft), and the slice brief the
   control room hands over.
2. The v1 skills being rebuilt, as the statement of behavior to keep:
   `plugins/sun/skills/sunrise/SKILL.md`, `plugins/sun/skills/sunset/SKILL.md` and
   `plugins/sun/assets/`.
3. `docs/plans/2026-09-13-recheck-v2-e8-core.md` section 6 (the A7a helper interface), and for the
   checkpoint and the write-ahead receipt, as the pattern for the run journal: its section 5 whole,
   and in its sections 4 and 12 every ruling and amendment that names either (E8-14, E8-28, E8-29,
   E8-A20, E8-A21 and E8-A45 among them). No heading of that plan names them (A38).
4. One finished v2 core as the pattern for a portable core with two adapters:
   `plugins/ship-v2/skills/ship-v2/` (`SKILL.md`, `references/`, `scripts/`, `adapters/`, `setups/`,
   `evals/seeded-cases/`), with its kill sweep as rewritten by the E15 punch list (E15 A34 (4)).
5. The E15 contract sections 3, 6 and 12 (the boundaries, the rulings, the shape of a seeded case)
   and A33 (the two runtimes side by side).
6. The guide `skills-best-practices.md` sections "Scripts", "The harness layer" and the pre-ship
   checklist, and the companion notes `skills-helper-recipes` and `skills-harness-notes`. The
   control room hands each builder a copy under its evidence folder; the vault is not a builder's
   to open.

**The wall.** No E16 agent opens or lists anything under any `answer-key/`, any
`trigger-set/held-out/`, any `routing-requests/` folder, any `trials/routing-*` prompt,
`~/.local/share/skills-v2-*`, any real `auth.json`, any real `.env*` file other than a
`.env.example`, or the real `~/.claude` or `~/.codex` by any spelling. A builder or the outside
reviewer also never opens `~/Developer/clerk/`, `~/ObsidianVault`, another lane's worktree or
scratch, and never runs a filesystem-wide search; every `find` prunes the wall. A fixture that
stands in for one of these (a made-up credentials file, a made-up vault) is built by the test under
its own temporary directory.

## 3. Boundaries every slice keeps

- One writer per worktree. A builder writes only under the folders its slice names (section 13) and
  the scratch directory it is given. Never `plugins/records/`, `plugins/readers/`, any of the ten
  v2 cores, any v1 plugin folder (`plugins/sun/` included), `.claude-plugin/marketplace.json` (the
  control room adds entries at close), `docs/feedback.md` or `docs/ideas.md`.
- A builder never runs `git add`, `commit`, `stash`, `checkout`, `reset`, `merge`, `worktree`,
  `branch`, or any git command that changes a worktree, its index or its branches. `status`, `log`,
  `diff`, `rev-parse`, `show`, `archive` to a temporary directory are fine. Git commands inside a
  test's own temporary repository are the test's. The control room checks every round and makes
  every commit.
- **No real service in slices 1 to 4.** No builder, test, proof or reviewer run calls a real `gh`,
  `vercel`, `supabase`, `psql` or `pg_dump` against an account, opens a real credentials store, or
  touches the real vault, the real memory stores, the real shortcut file, a real crontab or a real
  LaunchAgents folder. Every provider is its fake (E16-8); every machine location is a path under
  the test's temporary directory, reached through the one place the core resolves it (E16-12).
  The only real-service run of this step is slice 5, with the owner present.
- Runtime floor: `/usr/bin/python3` 3.9.6 and git 2.50.1. Python 3.9 syntax. Standard library
  only, with no exception (the owner's ruling, A47): the shipped tool starts in one way, `/bin/sh`
  running `/usr/bin/python3` by its full path with the session's settings cleared, and checks its
  own schema files with its own code (the frame's RUN-1).
- No network in any test. No MCP tool. No model call inside any script. A core's script never
  launches a harness and never invokes another skill.
- Tests build their fixtures in a temporary directory and clean up (`PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1`; no
  `__pycache__` left). Install probes run only in isolated homes. Suites are written so their
  modules run side by side from the first slice (P4).
- The repository is public. Fixtures are synthetic: no text copied from another repository, no
  personal, legal or financial content, no personal paths, no machine names, no account names, no
  key-shaped strings (a planted test secret is a marked canary built at run time, never a literal
  in a committed file that a scanner would read as a key).
- Test first for every behavior a check of section 12 or a required test names: the failing output
  is recorded before the code that makes it pass.
- No em dashes in anything written for this step, except inside a v1 form copied byte for byte
  where the form is ruled load-bearing (E16-13 names them).

## 4. Owner picks (RULED 2026-10-11: every pick as recommended; section 18, A1)

Each entry gives the pick the control room recommends and the alternative.

- **P0. The control room's model.** Pick: Opus 5.5 at high, acting as Clerk (the E14 punch list's
  and E15's practice). Alternative: Fable 5.1 at high.
- **P1. The closing full review.** Every hand-back is checked by the outside reviewer at LOW (R2).
  Pick: one full review at the close of the step, at LOW as in E15. Alternative: higher once, for
  that review only.
- **P2. Design pages before any builder.** Three hard classes, each drawn on a page and read by the
  outside reviewer at low before slice 1's builder starts: resuming a half-finished run across
  outside services; keeping credentials out of logs, traces and error output; undo and restore.
  Pick: yes (section 16; the three pages are drafted beside this document). Alternative: straight
  to the brief.
- **P3. Lanes.** Pick: slices 2, 3 and 4 side by side after the frame, three worktrees cut from
  the frame's commit, one writer each (section 13). Alternative: one lane in order.
- **P4. Suites side by side from the start.** Pick: every E16 suite is written so its modules run
  in parallel; E16 runs `ship-v2`'s suite once, at the close. Alternative: E15's layout.
- **P5. The check script.** Ruled in substance by R2 ("a script runs the suites"). Left to pick:
  one script runs the suites in both runtimes, compares counts and skips with the last good run,
  prints pass or fail, and no session watches a run by hand. Pick: yes, the punch-list round's
  script carried over.
- **P6. npm.** The owner asked what it is for. Measured: a label in one table of v1, never a step.
  Pick: drop the column and build no npm step. Alternative: a real publish operation for the
  library type (a new remote write with its own credentials, not in v1).
- **P7. Neon.** His provider list names Supabase and not Neon. Reading to confirm: sunrise
  provisions Supabase only, and `--db neon` goes. His Neon account still holds databases of
  existing projects, so sunset will meet them. Pick: sunset's backup is provider-blind (`pg_dump`
  on the direct connection string, R4's list notwithstanding), and its idle step says in the
  preview what it cannot do for a provider it has no integration for. Alternative: a Neon
  integration for sunset.
- **P8. Project types.** v1 has six: web app, monorepo, static site, Electron app, library, script.
  Pick: he names which stay; a type he drops is a refused input with a plain message, not a
  silent fallback.
- **P9. Plugin shape.** v1's `sun` plugin holds both skills because they share one `assets/`
  folder (an invariant of the repository's `AGENTS.md`). Pick: one `sun-v2` plugin with both
  skills and the shared lifecycle code. Alternative: two plugins with a copied frame.
- **P10. Layers kept from v1 besides the providers.** The local repo and the doc kit; staged-doc
  adoption; the vault entry; the two memory stores; the terminal shortcut; cancelling scheduled
  agents and crons on sunset; the closeout hand-off prompt; the sound and the one-line stamp at the
  start. Pick: all kept; he cuts any he no longer uses.
- **P11. Sunset's default without an expiry window.** Design page 3 lands the default. Pick: every
  record is preserved at a place with no clock (the archive folders on disk, the archived GitHub
  repository, a verified database backup) before any provider-side state that has a clock is
  entered, and the tombstone names each clock that still runs. Alternative: named by him.
- **P12. The live join.** Pick: one throwaway project under a name he approves, run with him
  present, taken through sunrise, sunset and restore in one sitting; the name and the accounts are
  in the preview he answers.
- **P13. The carried punch items' home. RULED** (2026-10-11, "1"): E18's pre-release round, named
  in the E15 contract's A34 (5).
- **P14. The v1 skills.** Pick: untouched through E16. The v1 `sun` plugin still names Notion; it
  is frozen, flagged, and not edited. Alternative: strike Notion from v1 now (a v1 edit).
- **P15. The leftover E15 worktrees.** Pick: remove `tony-skills-e15` and `tony-skills-e15-docs`
  on his word (both merged).
- **P16. Restore as a command.** The plan's artifact list says "restoration instructions" and its
  done-when says the checks demonstrate "restoration". v1's way back is a paragraph of prose. Pick:
  sunset writes a restore manifest beside its tombstone and a `restore` summons previews and runs
  it like any other run (wording changed by the owner's word, A6). Alternative: instructions only, the restoration demonstrated by hand in
  the join. This is new surface, so it is his call.

## 5. Owner rulings (given 2026-10-11, in the control room's windows, before this document)

- **R1, the order.** The E15 punch list lands before E16's code worktree is cut (E15 A6, and his go
  for the round). E16's paperwork is drafted while the punch list builds.
- **R2, the crew** (a review-policy change, D4, by his word). "Opus 5.5 High is the builder, and
  Astro Low is the checker" ("Astro" read as Astra) and, to the question whether Astra at low
  replaces the fresh Claude checker or sits beside it, "1.replaces". The recommended answer he
  took also said that a script runs the suites and that one full outside review stays at the close.
  The fresh checker's duties are carried by name in section 14.
- **R3, Notion is out.** "I don't use Notion anymore, and I don't want it spun up for Sunrise or
  Sunset. We can drop Notion altogether." No layer, no flag, no template line, no search.
- **R4, the providers.** "github, vercel, vault, supabase, not srue aobut npm, whats that for?"
  GitHub, Vercel, the vault and Supabase are in. npm is P6. Neon is P7.
- **R5, speed.** "Each one of these has taken an awfully long time, and I'm curious if we could
  speed it up but not lose the quality of what we're doing. If that's not possible, then we can
  continue to maintain the process that we have been doing." Read into P2, P3, P4 and P5, and into
  section 14's rule that a class which comes back a second time stops for him.
- **R6, the carried E15 items** go to E18's pre-release round (P13).
- Not ruled, a reading only: the owner's words on Gemini were not resolved; no Gemini row is on
  this step's crew.

## 6. Rulings of this contract

- **E16-1, behavior is kept, the mechanics move.** Sunrise keeps: the vault gate first; never
  clobber; preview before executing and an explicit go (how: the frame's RUN-8, RUN-9); ask what the project is and use his words;
  layers conditional on the project type; end verified (the push landed, the clean production
  address answers 200, the pulled env file holds its keys, the kit check passes); the cross-links
  written on day one; resumable (how: the frame's RUN-10, RUN-11, OP-5 to OP-7). Sunset keeps: the vault gate first; never delete; preview and an
  explicit go (how: the frame's RUN-8, RUN-9); ask the reason and use his words; cancel active automation; copy the project's own
  memory store before the repo moves, into the folder beside it (A2) (how: the frame's ORD-4, KEEP-3); back the database up before idling it and never drop
  it (how: the frame's KEEP-1 to KEEP-3); never move or archive a repo with uncommitted or unpushed work without surfacing it; the
  loose ends surfaced and never acted on. What moves is where the deterministic half lives (a
  script, not prose). What leaves is Notion (R3) and whatever the picks cut. A proposed change to
  any other decision is the owner's ruling and lands in section 18.
- **E16-2, frozen folders.** At close, `git diff --stat <branch point> -- plugins/records
  plugins/readers plugins/sun` and the same for the ten v2 core folders print nothing. A need those
  components do not meet is a numbered question and a stop on that point.
- **E16-3, the operation is the unit.** What one operation is, and what counts as its one change,
  is the frame's (`2026-10-12-stations-e16-frame.md`, OP-1, OP-2). A change that is not a row of the
  approved manifest does not happen (the frame's RUN-9). The frame
  document is the single source for the frame, and each lane's own rows for its operations; this
  contract restates neither (A5).
- **E16-4, the owner approves what he read, and nothing else is done.** The preview shows him every
  operation the run may make; nothing he was not shown is done after his go; and the plan he
  approved is never changed, a different plan needing a new preview and a new go. Each remote row is
  shown with the account it acts as, and a step only the owner can take is shown to him and never
  attempted by the script. How the preview, the go and the plan are bound together is the frame's
  (RUN-5, RUN-8, RUN-9, RUN-11, OP-9).
  This is the plan's "authorization named in their preview".
- **E16-5, a run keeps its record, and one run at a time works on a project.** The record is in the
  private folder the owner ruled (A2), outside every repository. How it is written, and how runs are
  kept apart, is the frame's (LOG-1, LOG-2, RUN-2 to RUN-4).
- **E16-6, resume.** A run that was killed or stopped can be gone on with. No effect is applied
  twice, and the tool never claims as its own what it cannot prove it did (A5); how it decides, and
  what may be made again without being an effect, is the frame's (OP-5 to OP-8, EV-1, EV-3). A kill at any
  point of any operation leaves a state a rerun completes or stops on plainly; the
  kill sweep of section 12 shows it for every row of every provider's fake.
- **E16-7, credentials never leave their channel** (the frame's SEC-1 to SEC-12). The tests show it
  on the success path and on
  every failure path (plan A8: "tests the tracing and error-output paths for credential
  exposure"); what they plant and where they look is the frame's SEC-11.
- **E16-8, providers sit behind one interface, each with a fake.** A provider module offers what
  the frame requires of one (its ROW-1, SEC-3 and SEC-4) and an identity read (its RUN-5); how the
  real one reaches its provider is the frame's (SEC-2, SEC-3). A fake is a program the
  tests put first on the path (the fake harness stays in this contract as a named exception, by the
  owner's word, A6): it keeps its state in a file, records every call's arguments and the
  names of the environment variables it was given, can be told to fail, to hang, to answer late and
  to echo a secret into its error text, and it can be killed mid-call. What v1 reached through a
  harness connector (the Vercel project list) the core reaches through the tool, never through a
  connector (a connector is not a declared command; the frame's SEC-3).
- **E16-9, declared git behavior.** Every git command that changes a repository or a remote is
  declared before the go, and none that was not declared is run (how, and how it is tested: the
  frame's OP-11; each lane's own list). D6 is read narrowly (the control room's proposal, with no typed word of the owner's; A6): sunrise pushes the initial scaffold to
  `main` of a repository this run created and whose remote holds no commit, once (the lane's push
  row; the frame's ORD-1, the made level, and OP-3, C4). If
  the real services force a tracked change after that push, the run stops for the owner (catalog
  Part 6, M1). Sunset's remote changes are a dated tag and the archive. No other push, and no push
  to `main` of a repository that already has history.
- **E16-10, sunset never deletes and its default leans on no clock** (A1, P11). Every record
  sunset undertakes to preserve is preserved and checked before anything is moved, removed or
  paused (in what order, and how it is checked again: the frame's ORD-4 and KEEP-1 to KEEP-3; the
  lane's rows); the tombstone
  names each provider clock that still runs (the lane's forms; the catalog's Part 6, M3).
- **E16-11, a way back is claimed only on proof** (A5), and restore is a run of its own, never
  sunset backwards (A1, P16; A6). What must be on record for a way back, and when, is the frame's
  (LOG-2, EV-4, EV-5, EV-7).
- **E16-12, the gate and the machine's facts.** The vault and machine gate keeps v1's
  four checks; it comes before anything else, the cue included, and a failed gate creates nothing
  (the frame's RUN-1).
  Every machine location (the developer root, the archive folder, the vault, the staging folder,
  the memory stores, the shortcut file) and every account name is resolved in one module, from a
  probe or from one user-level settings file outside any repository, and is overridable for tests
  (the machine-facts module stays in this contract as a named exception, by the owner's word, A6).
  No committed file holds a personal path or an account name.
- **E16-13, the executor decides, the script records.** A script does the deterministic work, by
  the frame's rules (RUN-1, RUN-5 to RUN-9, OP-5, LOG-1, FORM-1). It never chooses a name, a project type, a shortcut or a description, never answers
  for the owner, and never runs a scaffolder that was not a row he approved. The forms are v1's
  with exactly the changes the lane's list of form changes holds, the frame's lines among them
  (FORM-1 to FORM-3; the catalog's Part 5 is the draft of that list); any other change to a form is a stop.
- **E16-14, the harness seams are named.** What v1 did with a Claude Code tool or a model call
  lives in the adapter and is declared as a capability (when it is declared, and what follows where
  it is absent, is the frame's RUN-5, RUN-7 and OP-8):
  listing and cancelling scheduled routines, the two Claude memory stores, the kit check's canary
  line (the lane's kit-check row; the frame's OP-8). A capability that is absent on a harness is said so in the preview and in the
  summary, never passed silently.
- **E16-15, versions and interface.** The new plugin starts at `0.1.0` with `interface_version` 1
  in every CLI response. Every CLI follows the A7a helper interface (plan A8; the CLI interface
  stays in this contract as a named exception, by the owner's word, A6): `--help` with
  arguments, defaults, an example and side effects; JSON on standard output and nothing else;
  diagnostics on standard error; exit 0, 2, 3, 4, 10 and 1 with their A7a meanings; safe reruns;
  partial effects named in `--help`.
- **E16-16, no implicit import of v1.** A test in the new core fails on a path under `plugins/sun/`,
  on any deference to a v1 skill's text and on any subprocess whose arguments name a v1 skill. The
  shared cue assets are copied into the new plugin, not reached across.
- **E16-17, the frame freezes when slice 1 closes.** A lane that needs a frame change stops and
  asks; the control room makes the change once, in the frame's worktree, and every lane takes it.

## 7. Slice 1: the lifecycle frame

Built first, alone, in `plugins/sun-v2/`. The frame is what the frame document rules
(`2026-10-12-stations-e16-frame.md`, Part 3, groups 3.1 to 3.9) and the machinery the lanes' rows
run on. Beside it slice 1 builds: the
provider interface and the fake harness (E16-8); the gate and the machine-facts module (E16-12);
the CLI skeleton (E16-15), `skill-identity`, the example validator, the seeded-case driver, the
parallel suite layout (P4) and the kill-sweep helper.

Done when a run over fake rows can be looked, previewed, approved, executed, killed anywhere,
resumed, and scanned clean for a planted canary.

## 8. Sunrise, the local half (slice 2)

The lane's own rows, written against the frame (its ROW-1 to ROW-3) and read by the outside
reviewer before the lane's builder starts (A5). They start from the catalog's draft rows SR01 to
SR07, SR10, SR12 to SR14 and SR20 to SR25, the look steps G1 to G7, and sunrise's forms (catalog
Part 5, a draft).

## 9. The providers (slice 3)

One module per provider on the frame's interface (its SEC-3 and SEC-4), each with its fake (E16-8)
and an identity read that names the account in the preview. The lane's own rows are written against
the frame (its ROW-1 to ROW-3) and read by the outside reviewer before the lane's builder starts
(A5); they start from the catalog's draft rows: GitHub (SR08, SR15, T01, T20, V10); the hosting
provider (SR09, SR11, SR11a, SR16, T04, T21, V11); Supabase through the marketplace (SR17, SR17a,
SR17b, SR18, SR19, T22, V12); the provider-blind database backup (G9 and T03; A1, P7).

## 10. Sunset and restore (slice 4)

The lane's own rows, written against the frame (its ROW-1 to ROW-3) and read by the outside
reviewer before the lane's builder starts (A5). They start from the catalog's draft rows T02, T05,
T10 to T19 and T30, the look steps G8 and G10, restore's rows V01 to V09, V13 and V14, and their
forms (catalog Part 5, a draft). The barrier is the frame's KEEP-3; restore is its EV-5 to EV-7.

## 11. Slice 5: the join

The control room joins the three lanes on the frame's branch. Then: install proofs on both
harnesses in isolated homes; the whole sequence on fakes end to end (sunrise, kill and resume,
sunset, restore); the READMEs with measured counts; the marketplace entry and roster counts (the
control room's); guide findings; the live run of P12 with the owner present, from a preview he
answers, on a throwaway project that sunset retires and restore brings back in the same sitting;
then the full outside review (section 14).

## 12. Disposable-project checks

The plan's done-when names six demonstrations and one default. Each is a family of at least three
checks on a disposable project built from fakes. **Every family has a clean case, and the clean
case asserts the work was done, by name:** the files present with their bytes, the lines present
once, the fakes' state and call records, the journal's lines. A core that refuses everything, or
does nothing, fails every family on its clean case; the answer key holds the expected state and the
expected call list of each case, not only the word "refused". Expected outcomes go behind the wall
in the packet's `answer-key/` and the control room grades.

| Family | Demonstrates | Clean case asserts | Planted condition | What a pass looks like |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| L0 the verified baseline | v1's "end verified" (E16-1) | one sunrise per kept project type, end to end: the remote's head is the local head, the clean address answers 200 for that commit, the env file holds its key names, the kit check passes (on a harness without the canary capability the canary line is `unavailable` and the summary says so; the frame's OP-8), every local layer written | (the clean case is the family) | each of the four proofs is a result line with the value it checked |
| L1 the gate | vault and machine preflight | the gate passes on a sound fixture vault and the run goes on to its preview | no vault; a stub without its marker file; a vault inside a synced documents folder | the run ends before any operation; nothing created, the cue not played (the frame's RUN-1) |
| L2 never clobber | preflight | on a clear machine every candidate operation is planned and none is stopped on (the frame's RUN-7), and the preview lists every operation | an existing local folder; an existing remote; a taken project name on the host; a shortcut that shadows a program; the owner's own identical line already present | the run ends naming each; nothing written; the identical line is reported as found already so, with no way back (the frame's RUN-7, EV-4) |
| L3 adoption | staged-document adoption | one staged scope doc moved under its date, the source gone, the hash equal | two candidates for one role; a doc an interrupted run already moved; a doc with no date anywhere | asked, nothing moved; reported as already moved, never moved a second time, and said to be proven the run's or not as the frame decides (OP-6, EV-1); asked |
| L4 credentials | credential-safe execution | the env file and the dump exist, owner-only, and hold the canary; nothing else does | a canary in each source the frame's SEC-1 names; every failure path: a fake that fails echoing it whole, in part, encoded and split; a fake that writes it to its own log; a kill mid-call; a missing tool; an expired token; an empty or half env pull; a redirect; a malformed answer; a full disk; a linked output path; an exception; a debug variable in the session | the canary, whole, in part or encoded, and any value a fake issued during the run, is nowhere but where the frame lets a secret rest or travel (SEC-2, SEC-7); how that is looked for, and what the fake host may record, is the frame's SEC-11 |
| L5 git | declared Git behavior | every git command that changed a repository or a remote was declared in the manifest, a scaffolder's writes included, as the frame's OP-11 tests it, **and** agrees with the key's expected list; one push, nothing after | a remote that already has a commit; a tracked edit needed after the push; a secret in a commit to be pushed | refused; stopped for the owner, no second push; stopped before the push |
| L6 resume | resumable partial failure | an unkilled run's journal and end state, as the reference | a kill at every point of every operation of every fake; a lost create response; a torn last line; two runs at once | the rerun ends in the reference end state, or in that state with an operation listed as not proven where the frame says so (OP-6, EV-1), or stops plainly; in every case no effect is applied twice (a check may be made again; the frame's OP-8); after a lost create response there is one resource, never two, and the create is handled as the frame's OP-7 says; the second run ends on the lock (the frame's RUN-2) |
| L7 restore | restoration | after sunset the retired state is asserted on every layer; after restore every layer is back, byte-equal where the bytes are ours, the copies still in the archive | an expired provider state; a line someone edited after sunset; the old name taken; a kill mid-restore | said so with the owner step; stopped on the edit, nothing changed; stopped; the rerun completes, or stops plainly, or lists an operation as not proven, as the frame decides (OP-6, EV-1), and applies no effect twice |
| L8 no clock | sunset's default | a default sunset: the dump present, verified and bound to the idled database; every provider fake in its retired state; the tombstone lists each clock | a dump cut off; a dump of another database; a failed tag push; a dump that cannot be bound | for the first three, the run stops before anything is retired, with every fake live and every path where it was (the dumps by the frame's KEEP-1 to KEEP-3, the tag push by the order of its phases, ORD-4); for the last, the outcome the frame's KEEP-2 gives: the dump kept, no idle call made, the preview saying so, and the rest of the run going on |
| L9 the preview binds | the authorization named in the preview | an approved manifest runs every operation it holds and no other | a manifest changed after the go; an operation outside it; an owner operation (the frame's OP-9) | refused; refused; shown to him and never attempted, the run ending `waiting` unless the tool's own read already shows the step done (the frame's OP-9) |
| T1 no v1 import | E16-16 | the test passes on the core | a planted v1 path in a scratch copy | the test fails on the copy |

## 13. Slices, crew, worktrees

| Slice | Builds | Builder | Where | Checked by |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | this document and the picks applied (the control room); the frame (a new author, A5) | as named | `tony-skills-e16-docs`, `docs/stations-e16` | the owner; the frame by the outside reviewer at low |
| 1 | section 7 | Opus 5.5, high | `tony-skills-e16`, `feat/stations-e16`, cut from `main` after the E15 punch list merges | the script (P5), the outside reviewer at low, the control room |
| 2 | section 8 | Opus 5.5, high | `tony-skills-e16-rise`, cut from slice 1's commit | same |
| 3 | section 9 | Opus 5.5, high | `tony-skills-e16-prov`, cut from slice 1's commit | same |
| 4 | section 10 | Opus 5.5, high | `tony-skills-e16-set`, cut from slice 1's commit | same |
| 5 | section 11 | the control room joins; one builder for the join's fixes | `tony-skills-e16` | same, then the owner present for the live run, then the full review |

Slices 2, 3 and 4 run side by side if P3 is ruled so. Each lane writes only the files its brief
names; the frame is frozen (E16-17). Loop per hand-back: brief; the builder builds; the script runs
the suites; the outside reviewer checks; one fix round if needed; the control room commits exactly
the tree that was checked.

Every builder reports: files written; the tests and the verbatim tail of their output, with the
failing output that came first; every question the contract left it, numbered, with the section and
the reading it took; what it saw and did not touch; guide findings; and the hand-back markers
(`report.md`, `out/progress.txt`, `out/ALL.done`). A builder that finds this contract wrong says so
and stops on that point.

## 14. Review and close

**Every hand-back.** The fresh checker of E13 to E15 is replaced by the outside reviewer at low
(R2), and its duties are carried by name: (a) her own list first, written from this contract and
the brief before she reads the builder's tests; (b) the full suites in both runtimes after the
builder's last edit, the run that counts, run by the script the control room launches, each runtime
with its own temporary directory, log and exit file; (c) her sealed copy matched to the checked
tree file by file by sha256; (d) `/usr/bin/python3` with `jsonschema` from the user site in her
sandbox. The control room's own check, every round: the diff read; its own probe of each new
behavior through the real CLI on fakes; the section 12 checks graded against the key; the frozen
folders unchanged (E16-2); no `__pycache__`; a scan for personal paths, machine names, account
names and key-shaped strings; the hand-back verified by time and by tree fingerprint. Then one
local commit of exactly the tree that was checked, proven file by file.

**Severity and rounds.** Only a BLOCKER, or a MAJOR a good-faith run could trigger, opens a fix
round. A class that comes back a second time stops for the owner to name a design step (E15
P11 (c), R5).

**The full review (after slice 5).** At P1's effort, on the finished step: the plan's done-when
claims, the seam with the v1 plugin left in place, the credential guard on every path, the live
run's record. One fix round, one verification round on the closed list, then close under plan
ruling 17: no BLOCKER open, every MAJOR fixed or explicitly carried to the step that builds the
thing and written down, every suite green. Her verdicts go in her report files and in chat, never
on the board.

No push, no PR, no merge to `main` without the owner's typed word for each.

## 15. Reporting

The control room records the build in the new plugin's `README.md` with measured counts, adds the
marketplace entry and corrects the roster counts at close, appends guide findings to
`docs/guide-findings.md` under `## Inbox`, files the briefs, the builders' reports and the
reviewer's outputs in the audit packet under `astra-outputs/e16/` (with
`reports/control-room-notes.md`), writes the board through its helper at each stage change with the
Board window messaged each time, writes the closing report to `~/Documents/handoffs/`, and closes
this document with section 18.

## 16. What is still open

The design questions the first pages raised are ruled (A2: Q1, Q3, Q4, Q5) or carried as
measurements in the catalog's Part 6 (M1, the old Q2; M3, the old Q6; M2 is withdrawn by A6). The
catalog's Part 6 names the risk that is left: nine risks (RK1 to RK8 and RK10; RK9 is merged into
RK7, A30; RK8 reaches anything a run holds as safe, A32, and covers any run, A33; RK10 is a record
of the tool's own that cannot be read, A40) and the run directory's assumption, each accepted by
the owner (section 18, A4, A6, A18, A26, A28, A30, A32, A33 and A40). The frame author's questions are ruled (A6, A8, A9, A11, A18). Still open: five cases the frame's author sorted under the
governing rule that no ruling names (A12), and one more (A15); the outside reviewer's check of
the rules on secrets as written under the turned default (A20, A21, A22), which she found
wanting (A24) and, after the fix rounds, wanting again in one class (A32): by the owner's ruling
a narrow fix by the frame's author, with no further check on paper, her closing rules checked as
code in the first slice; that fix is handed back in two rounds (A33), the author's question PS14
is ruled (A34) and written in (A35), its question PS15 is ruled (A35) and written in (A36), and
nothing more was owed by the author then; the first slice's first hand-back, which the reviewer's check
of the code sent back in that same class, a sixth return, with the gaps the build found in the
frame, and the owner's ruling on the next step (A38); the stop A22 names that no ruling covers (the sunset look's question is
ruled, A23); the keying-file stop, kept and not ruled (A25, A32); M1 and M3.

## 17. What this step never does

Retire, edit or rename the v1 `sun` plugin; touch Notion; call a real service outside slice 5's
owner-present run; delete a repository, a project, a database, a vault note or a memory note;
push to `main` of a repository with history; push, open a PR or merge in this repository without
the owner's words; read a secret aloud; publish anything.

- **A6, 2026-10-12, the frame, and the owner's answers to its author's questions.** A new author (a fresh session
  of the building model) wrote `docs/plans/2026-10-12-stations-e16-frame.md` from the outside reviewer's three
  reports: 62 rules, each with one test line, nine kinds of operation, seven constructions, nine worked examples.
  The control room checked it against sections 5 and 18 and against nothing else, and committed it as handed
  back. The author decided none of its open points and put sixteen questions to the owner (O1 to O16); the control room added two of its own. Each was
  put to him alone, the recommended option first; his typed words follow each. The frame's own rules are changed
  by its author, never by the control room.
  - **O1, a move.** "May the tool move folders by itself?" 1. Yes, as a fourth named risk. 2. No, I do the moves.
    He typed "Yes, as a fourth named risk". **RK4, accepted:** the tool reads which folder is at a path, then
    renames it; a folder put in its place between the read and the rename is moved; the tool sees that straight
    afterwards and stops with both places named; nothing is deleted. How likely it is was not measured. This
    does not give a way back for a move whose record was lost to a kill (O11).
  - **O6, the scope of RK2.** "Does the file-swap risk you accepted cover every file the tool rewrites?" 1. Yes,
    every file swap. 2. No, shared files only, as first worded. He typed "1", told that the frame was already
    written so, that the author's count of the rows it adds was not verified, and that a save lost in a swap is
    silent. **RK2, restated:** when the tool rewrites any file it checks that the file is unchanged, then swaps
    the new version in; a program that writes the same file between those two steps, without taking the lock,
    loses its write, and the tool does not notice.
  - **O2, read-then-ask beyond RK3's four rows.** "May the tool also read, then ask at a provider in places beyond
    the four you accepted?" 1. No, keep these as my steps for now. 2. Yes, extend the risk to them. He typed "1":
    not extended. Connecting the repository to the hosting project, and cancelling or recreating a harness
    routine through the adapter, are his steps, shown and read back, unless the provider's own refusal on a
    changed state is cited. Told with it: RK3 covers the same change made by another in the instant and says
    nothing of a different one. A departure from v1 for the routine: the run still sees it cancelled (E16-1), by
    his hand.
  - **O3, a crontab.** "May the tool edit your crontab?" 1. No, I edit the crontab. 2. Yes, as a named risk. He
    typed "1": the tool does not. Removing a project's line on sunset and putting it back on restore are his
    steps, read back. A departure from v1.
  - **O4, a shortcut's name.** "May the tool add the project's terminal shortcut by itself?" 1. Yes, as a named
    risk. 2. No, I add it. He typed "1. Yes, as a named risk". **RK5, accepted, and no wider than this:** the
    tool checks that no program has the shortcut's name, then adds the line, in sunrise and when a restore puts
    it back; a program of that exact name
    installed between the check and the add is hidden by the shortcut until the line is removed; it shows the
    first time the name is typed; nothing is lost. Not measured. It does not open the class of conditions on
    something the effect does not change.
  - **O5, the frame's one assumption.** "Do you accept the one thing the whole frame rests on?" 1. Yes, accept
    it. 2. No, send it back. He typed "1". Accepted: nothing but the run changes its run directory or a staging
    folder. The tool checks that the folder is owner-only and that its log is unaltered partway; it cannot detect
    a clean rewrite from end to end by a program running as the owner.
  - **O7, a create whose answer was lost.** "When a create request gets no answer, should the tool stop and never
    resend it by itself?" 1. Yes, stop. 2. No, the tool should recover alone. He typed "1". For a thing whose
    name the provider keeps unique, a new plan with a new go may send again; for any other, the tool never
    creates again. A departure from v1, which retries. This does not decide adoption (O10) or a create that
    carries the run's mark (O8).
  - **O8, a create that carries the run's mark.** "Should the tool tag what it creates, so it can recognize its
    own work after a lost answer?" 1. No, leave it out for now. 2. Yes, have the author add it. He typed "1":
    not added.
  - **O10, what may be adopted.** "Besides a local folder, may you point sunrise at something that already
    exists and have it use that?" 1. No, only the local folder, as today. 2. Yes, a database too. 3. Yes, any of
    the three. He typed "nevermind.  keep it to answer #1", read as option 1 of that question only. An existing
    remote repository, hosting project or database in the way is a stop he clears by hand. A departure from
    v1's "offer to adopt". Told with it: a lost database answer then leaves only dropping that layer.
  - **O9, the one push to a repository he names: moot, not ruled.** After O10 its case cannot arise. What stands
    is E16-9 as proposed by the control room, with no typed word of his: the push goes only to a repository this
    run created and whose remote holds no commit.
  - **O11, an effect that landed with no record of it.** "What should restore do with a step it cannot prove
    sunset did?" 1. Restore lists it and undoes it only if you say so for that line. 2. Restore never offers to
    undo it. He typed "1". The words "accepted on purpose" in the catalog's withdrawn Part 2 were the control
    room's, never his.
  - **The restore manifest (the control room's question).** P16 as put on 2026-10-11 said "sunset writes a

    the pick's words. He typed "1". No restore manifest is written at sunset; the pick's wording changes by his

  - **O12, a move through the tool's own holding folder.** 1. No, move them directly. 2. Yes, have the author

  - **O13, a cancelled schedule in the tombstone.** "How should the tombstone record a cancelled schedule?"

  - **O14, "leave it".** "May you tell sunset to leave a step of yours undone and still get the tombstone?"
    1. Yes. 2. No, the tombstone waits for every step. He typed "1". Told with it: the cost named at A4's

  - **O16, how a step of his completes.** "How should the tool know a step of yours is done?" 1. Check it where

    frame. No slice has the owner's go.

    choice. The run stops and stays open, so that a new plan with a new go can still mend it; it does not end
    with exceptions, and no last record is written until it is resolved. Where the step cannot run because of
    his own answer or his own "leave it" (A6, O14), the run ends and lists it, as before. The frame is to be

- **A12, 2026-10-12, the frame after A11's change; five cases its author sorted that no ruling names.** The
  frame's author changed two rules, ORD-1 and ORD-2, each in its rule text and its test line, and nothing else
  (65 rules, 65 test lines; no example changed). A reader who wrote neither compared the two versions rule by
  rule: the same two rules and no other. The control room committed the frame as handed back.
  **What the frame now says.** ORD-1 holds one table: each of the thirteen final statuses against each of the
  four levels of need, 52 cells, each `meets`, `stop`, `his` or `branch`. When a need can no longer be met:
  if any provider's cell is `stop`, the run stops with the reason "need unmet", the operation gets no status,
  the run stays open and no last record is written; otherwise, if any is `his`, the operation is recorded
  `held` and the run goes on and ends with exceptions; otherwise every provider is a branch not taken, and
  the run stops. ORD-2 says how a new plan mends such a stop: the need is settled by the treatment its row
  declares (the operation left out, or the need dropped), the new preview says which and the status that
  caused it, and nothing runs before a new go.
  **Ruled cells.** `not proven` against a need for a thing the run made: stop (A11). An answer of another
  value, and `left`: the run ends and lists it (A11; A6 at O14). `held` follows from those two.
  **Not ruled: the author's reading under the governing rule.** Five cases are written as a stop because the
  governing rule says stop where no ruling says otherwise. None is the owner's ruling. (a) The provider was
  found already so, and the need is for a thing this run made. (b) The provider is a step the owner did
  himself, and the need is for a thing this run made. (c) A check was not available on this harness and the
  need does not accept that. (d) A step was done on the owner's word with no read to check it, and the need
  does not accept his word. (e) Every branch of the plan ended refused or not needed, and none provided the
  thing. The author notes that (c) and (d) are known before the go and could be settled in the preview
  instead. A status at a level it can never meet (a thing done where a check's pass is needed) also stops;
  that is a defect of a lane's row.
  **The count of stops.** A7 records 22 places where the frame stops for the owner and v1 goes on. After this
  change it is 23, by the author's count: the unmet need is a named stop again. How many draft rows meet it
  depends on the level a lane gives each need; the author names one, the push after a commit not proven.
  **Seen and left.** FORM-2's line for a stop names "the command he can run"; a "need unmet" stop has no
  command, and ORD-1 says what the form names instead. FORM-2 was left as it is (A9). By the author's search
  and the control room's, no sentence of this contract outside section 18 states the ending that was removed;
  L6 and L7 accept a rerun that stops plainly.
  **Not yet done:** the outside reviewer's second read of the frame. No slice has the owner's go.

  closed before is open again. Her last line: a blocker stands. Across six reads: 18, 20, 21, 15, 9, 5
  findings; BLOCKER 11, 8, 8, 1, 1, 1.
  **What stands.** D8: the frame rebuilds its register of secrets on every later invocation of a run; a
  restore's first invocation is not a later invocation of anything, so it can still take a secret the
  retirement sealed as safe to show. She calls it a further return of a class she had already marked
  returned. Over three reads the same class appeared in a provider's address, then across a resume, then
  across runs. J1, new: the same fix stops a whole restore where one sealed record cannot be read, where
  EV-7 withholds only the reverses that depend on it. H5: a refusal at a failed gate is given a form that
  RUN-1 forbids creating. H13: the repair write EV-7 orders conflicts with RUN-12's rule that a refused
  summons changes nothing. H12: two cells of ROW-1's table conflict with OP-9 and RUN-6. She also names three
  changes of the round that her closing rules did not require; each is inside J1, H13 or H12.
  **The stop.** Before the check began the control room wrote down how its result would be counted: D8 or
  D14 marked returned again is a class back a second time, and section 14's stop fires at any severity. It
  fired on D8. Put to the owner as "The secret leak came back a second time. What is the next step?" 1. One
  more round, deeper check. 2. The same round, her check at low. 3. Start the first builder. 4. Something
  else you name. He typed "1". So, as his design step: the frame's author states the rule on secrets once,
  for every invocation and every place a value can be written, and fixes J1, H5, H12 and H13; the reviewer
  then checks the rules on secrets and the changed rules at medium effort, for this check, where every read
  until now was at low (A1). Told with it: what medium costs was not measured, and it is no promise.
  **A15 is corrected on one count.** It says that before a first go an invocation "writes no manifest and no
  journal". That is true of a refusal only: an invocation that ends with its preview has written the
  manifest the preview shows (RUN-8). The reviewer found this. **Added to A15's record:** the author's note
  on a sealed secret kept only in a saved copy does not reopen D8, by her reading: SEC-4 already sends a
  sealed result straight to a protected record, so a lane that did otherwise would break that rule. The
  author's reading recorded in A15 as not ruled (the stop when a save fails after his go) she finds
  consistent with the governing rule; it is still not his ruling.
  **Not yet done:** the third fix round; the check at medium. No slice has the owner's go.
- **A17, 2026-10-12, the frame after the third fix round.** The frame's author answered the five findings
  A16 names. A reader who wrote neither compared the two versions rule by rule: seven rules changed in their
  rule text (RUN-1, RUN-2, RUN-12, EV-7, SEC-4, SEC-12, ROW-1), five of them also in their test line (all but
  RUN-1 and SEC-4); no example changed; no rule added, removed or renumbered (65 rules, 65 test lines). The
  control room committed the frame as handed back.
  **The rule on secrets, stated once (D8).** SEC-12 no longer speaks of earlier and later invocations. An
  invocation that has passed the gate and taken a key's lock opens every run directory ever made under that
  key, before its first use of a runner, and its register then holds every secret those runs sealed. Until
  that is done it runs nothing, parses nothing and writes only the tool's own fixed text. The rule holds a
  table: eleven ways an invocation can begin, each with the rules that allow it, against seven places a
  value can go; each cell says the place is reached only after the opening, or why nothing parsed can reach
  it. Two limits the rule states of itself are below.
  **A sealed record that cannot be read (J1).** One outcome, in every invocation and not only in a restore:
  the invocation goes on; while it runs it takes no id, name, hash or part of an address from a provider as
  safe by its declared meaning, only where it equals something already on record, and seals the rest; every
  form and the output say that a sealed record is unavailable and that values are withheld. What a restore
  offers is still EV-7's to decide. The stop the second fix round had put there is gone. The cost, by the
  author's count of draft rows: while such a record is unavailable, an operation that needs a new id from a
  provider stops under the ordinary rules (five rows: four creates and the recreating of a routine).
  **The three others.** H5: a refusal at the gate or at a held lock is the tool's fixed text in its output
  and creates nothing; a refusal form is a file only once a run directory may exist. H13: the closing record
  of a retirement that a restore has closed is written after the gate, the locks and the checks of the run
  directory, and before the invocation refuses or runs anything. H12: an owner answer's mode comes from its
  kind, and the planning test of a check or an owner's row is the frame's general one; ROW-1 says both.
  **For the owner to see.** (1) Every invocation, a first look included, now reads into memory every secret
  that any earlier run under the same project keys sealed; the register is written nowhere. (2) The outcome
  for an unreadable sealed record reaches a sunrise and a sunset, where the round before stopped them. (3) A
  look may no longer run a command that returns a secret; such a command belongs inside an operation. (4) A
  project name that appears only in a provider's answer is never used to take a lock. The count of stops is
  24, unchanged, by the author's count.
  **Two limits, put to the owner and not ruled here.** (N5) Where a sealed record is lost for good, every
  later invocation under that project's key withholds provider values again, and no rule ends it. (N6) A
  channel secret that was replaced between two invocations is not in any register, since the tool drops a
  channel secret after use (A2), so the old value would not be recognised if a provider repeated it. The
  frame states both and accepts neither for him.
  **Seen and left, by the author's report.** The checks of a run directory (owner-only, the journal's chain)
  are written for the invocation's own; no rule says they are made on the other run directories the
  invocation now opens. RUN-3 does not say where its lookup of open runs sits in RUN-1's order. A summons
  that only renders status ends in a way RUN-12's list does not name. FORM-2 has no line for a withheld
  value; by A13 that is the lane's list's to give.
  **Not yet done:** the owner's answers on N5 and N6; the outside reviewer's check, at medium. No slice has
  the owner's go.
- **A18, 2026-10-12, the owner's rulings on the two limits of the rule on secrets.** Each was put to him
  alone, the recommended option first.
  - **A token that was replaced (N6).** "If you replace a provider token, should the tool keep a private copy
    of the old one so it can still spot it?" 1. No, accept the limit. 2. Yes, keep private copies. He typed
    "1". **RK6, accepted:** the tool reads a channel secret, uses it and keeps no copy (A2), so a token he has
    since replaced is in no record the tool holds; if a provider repeats the old token, the tool does not
    recognise it and may write it where it can be read. Told with it: a replaced token is usually dead, not
    always; how likely a provider is to repeat one was not measured. The catalog's Part 6 now holds RK6.
  - **A sealed record lost for good (N5).** "If a sealed record is lost for good, should your recorded word
    that the secret is dead end the withholding?" 1. Yes, my word ends it. 2. No, it stays as written. He
    typed "1". So: where a record that held a sealed secret cannot be read, his recorded word that the lost
    value is dead, replaced at the provider, ends the withheld state for that record; without his word it
    lasts. Told with it: this rests on his word; if the value is not dead and a provider repeats it, the
    tool could show it. The control room had first told him this point would stay as written, as a notice;
    it corrected that to him and put the question, since the state as written had no way out.
  - **Owed by the frame's author, before the reviewer's check:** RISK-1 names six risks; SEC-12 says of the
    replaced token that the limit is accepted (RK6) and says how his word ends the withheld state.
  - **The control room's slip in recording this:** the commit before this one (`660011a`) carries the
    catalog's RK6 alone, under a message that also names this entry and section 16; a script stopped before
    it wrote this file and the commit went on. This commit carries what that message promised.
  - **Not yet done:** the frame's change; the outside reviewer's check, at medium. No slice has the owner's
    go.
- **A19, 2026-10-12, the frame after the owner's two answers.** The frame's author changed two rules, SEC-12
  and RISK-1, each in its rule text and its test line (65 rules, 65 test lines; no example). A reader who
  wrote neither compared the two versions rule by rule: the same two. The control room committed the frame
  as handed back.
  **His word on a lost record (A18, N5).** He names the lost record as the forms name it. His word is taken

  with a first look. It is recorded, after the run directories are opened and checked and before anything
  runs, in the journal of the run that held the record, as a word like his go; the frame needed no new kind
  of operation and no new kind of record. A word for a record that can be read, or for none found
  unreadable, is not taken. One word ends the withheld state for one record; another lost record keeps it
  until it has its own. SEC-12 says, in the words told to him, that after his word the tool does not

  **RK6 (A18, N6).** SEC-12 says the limit on a replaced channel secret is accepted; RISK-1 names six risks

  **Changed by the control room in this entry's commit:** the catalog's Part 6 gains what he accepted with
  his word on a lost record, in the words told to him, so that everything he accepted is worded in one
  place.
  **Seen and left, by the author's report.** His word may be written into the journal of a run that has
  already ended; no rule forbids a record after a run's ending, and none says it may. FORM-2 has no line
  for a record lost and ended by his word; by A13 that is the lane's list's to give.

- **A20, 2026-10-12, the outside reviewer's check at medium; the stop, a second time; the owner's design
  step; A17 corrected.** She checked at medium, on a sealed copy at `6888a56`: the eight rules that had

  MINOR (K4, K5, K6), equal to her summary line. Of the five the round was to close: H5, H13 and H12 CLOSED;
  D8 RETURNED, a third time; J1 OPEN, and raised from major to blocker. Nothing she had closed before is

  is the first read at medium.
  **What stands.** D8, two sequences: a command that issues a secret is run again after a kill and issues a
  different one, which is lost before it is saved, while the earlier record for that command remains and so

  invocation stopped before it has opened its run directories may still write "safe content already on
  record". J1: while a sealed record cannot be read the frame withholds ids, names, hashes and parts of an
  address, and still takes a count as safe, so a lost numeric value repeated as a count gets through. K7:
  the third fix round had RUN-2 take no lock for a project name that appears only in a provider's answer, so
  two summonses for one project under different spellings can miss each other's lock. K4, K5: two places
  where the new opening contradicts an older rule. K6: the owner's word on a lost record is tested on one of
  its five summonses. She says that neither RK6 nor what he accepted with his word (A18) covers D8 or J1.
  **The stop.** Written down before the check began: D8 marked returned again fires section 14's stop at any

  1. Change the approach on secrets. 2. One more fix round. 3. Prove it in code. 4. Something else you name.
  He typed "1". So, as his design step: on the rules on secrets alone, the default is turned around. Until
  now a value a provider returns is taken as safe unless the tool recognises it as a secret; from now it is

  first puts to him what may count as proven safe, each with what he would then see and not see; he rules;

  fact. (2) A17 lists, among things for the owner to see, that a project name appearing only in a provider's

  minor findings with them; the check at medium. No slice has the owner's go.
- **A21, 2026-10-12, the owner's rulings on what counts as proven safe.** The frame's author changed no file
  and returned a plan and five questions. Its plan: a value a provider returns is held unless one of three
  grounds proves it safe: it is a fixed word from a list in the command's declaration; it equals something
  safe by where it came from (the owner typed it, the tool sent it, or the tool made or measured it); or it
  equals something the owner wrote in his settings file. No type of value is taken as safe by its shape.
  **How he ruled.** The first question was put to him alone and in full; he was told there were five and
  given one line on each of the others. He typed "go with oyru recs for all 5". So each of the five is ruled
  at the option recommended. The other four were then told to him in full, each with its cost; any of them
  is his to change by a word.
  - **PS1.** "When a provider repeats back exactly what you typed or what the tool sent, may the tool show
    it?" 1. Yes, show it. 2. No, show nothing. Ruled 1: a provider's value is shown where it equals what he
    typed, what the tool sent in that request, or what the tool built itself; what differs is not shown and
    a private file is named. A value is safe only by where it came from, never because a provider returned it.
  - **PS2, the account the tool acts as.** Ruled 1: he writes his account and team names once in his settings
    file on this machine; an account is shown only where the provider's answer equals one of them; where the
    tool is logged in as any other, it stops and does not show what the provider reported. Cost told: a
    one-time entry, and a stop where v1 goes on. Not taken: showing what the provider reports, as a seventh
    named risk.
  - **PS3, a thing found at a provider under a name he did not type.** Ruled 1: it is held, and the preview
    says what kind of thing it is, how many, and names a private file that lists them; where he types the
    name, it is his own word and is shown from then on. Cost told: in a sunset he approves some lines by
    opening a file. Not taken: shown as reported, as a named risk.
  - **PS4, a provider shows the project under another name** (the reviewer's K7). Ruled 1: the look stops
    and says so; he summons again and gives that name too; both names are then his typed words and both are
    locked before anything runs. Cost told: one more summons where a project's names differ. Not taken: a
    lock taken unseen under the provider's name.
  - **PS5, his word on a lost record (A18).** Under the turned default every value from a provider is held
    unless proven, so there is no separate state for his word to end. Ruled 1: the frame no longer takes that
    word, and what he accepted with it is withdrawn from the risk record. A18's ruling on a replaced token
    (RK6) stands; the frame's author says it has less to cover, since a token repeated in a provider's answer
    is now held like any other value.
  **Told to him with the first question.** A held value cannot be handed to a command as an argument. The
  author found no draft row that needs a provider's own id that way and did not test the tools; a reading
  of v1's two skills found none either. Where a lane finds such a tool, the step comes back to him before
  it becomes his (ROW-2). Also told: under the plan the tool no longer reads every earlier secret at each
  start.
  **Not yet done:** the rules on secrets written under these rulings, with the reviewer's six findings of
  A20; the catalog's Part 6 changed to agree (the entry for his word on a lost record marked withdrawn, RK6
  worded for what it still covers); the check at medium. No slice has the owner's go.
- **A22, 2026-10-12, the rules on secrets under the turned default, as the frame's author handed them; a
  sixth question and a stop no ruling names; the risk record changed to agree.** This is the control
  room's record of what the frame now says. It does not say that the rules hold: the outside reviewer
  has not read them.
  **What was handed back.** The frame alone, committed exactly as handed (`2dde7d0`). By a comparison
  made rule by rule by a reader who wrote neither version: 19 rules changed, none added or removed, no
  id moved, 65 rules and 65 test lines. Rule text and test line: SEC-1, SEC-2, SEC-3, SEC-4, SEC-6,
  SEC-7, SEC-12, RUN-1, RUN-2, RUN-12, ROW-1. Rule text alone: SEC-11, OP-9, LOG-2, EV-1, EV-7, KEEP-1,
  ROW-2. Test line alone: RUN-3. One cell of Example 3. Against the author's own list in its plan: ROW-2
  changed and was not on it; RUN-13 and RISK-1 were on it and did not change. The author's context was
  compacted in the middle of the work; it says it read the rules on secrets and its difference again
  before it reported.
  **What the frame now says.** SEC-4: no value is safe because of its shape; a value a provider returns
  is held unless one of three grounds proves it safe (a fixed word on the declaration's list; equality
  with content safe by where it came from; equality with a name in the owner's settings file); a table
  gives each of ten types of result its ground or says it is held, and a time is never taken from a
  provider. A held value may be compared in memory, rest in a protected record, go back to the provider
  that issued it, go to a child as a protected input, or be written to a file SEC-7 names for it, and
  nothing else. SEC-12: the register is a backstop for this invocation's own secrets and reaches back
  into no earlier record; each execution of a command with a secret field writes that a receipt is due
  before it starts and gives the receipt after; until the gate, the locks, the look for an open run and
  the checks of the run directory have passed, an invocation writes fixed text and a run id it made, and
  nothing read from a journal, a manifest or a record; one table names 17 places a value may be written
  and one names 12 ways an invocation may begin. The opening of every earlier run directory at each
  start is gone, and so is the withheld state. RUN-2 carries PS4, SEC-6 carries PS3, SEC-4 carries PS1
  and PS2, and the frame no longer names the owner's word on a lost record (PS5).
  **The reviewer's six findings, by the author's account, not yet checked.** D8, both halves: the
  receipt for each execution, and fixed text only before the startup checks pass. J1: a lost secret a
  provider returns is held like any other value, in all ten types. K7: stopped, by PS4. K4: a refusal at
  the first key and at a later key stated apart, both before a run directory is opened or made. K5: what
  each kind of summons reads at its start, with a close reading nothing. K6: nothing left to exercise; a
  search of the frame for the retired word finds none.

    value received in a field declared secret that equals content already recorded as safe stops the
    run, since it means the tool's own declaration of that provider's answer is wrong (by the author's
    count three rows carry such a field: SR17, SR17b, T04). The author put it under the governing rule
    and says it is cheap to drop. Kept, at the control room's recommendation, under the owner's sentence
    of this date (below); his to change by a word.
  - **PS6, a sixth question from the author: a provider clock that began before the run.** Where a
    resource was already paused or idled before the sunset, only the provider knows when its clock
    began, and a time from a provider is held. 1. The tombstone names the clock and what is lost, says
    the tool does not know when it began, and says where to read the date at the provider (recommended;
    the frame as handed). 2. The tombstone shows the provider's reported date, which needs a fourth
    ground, for times. Taken at 1 under the same sentence; his to change by a word. A1's P11 has the
    tombstone name each clock still running and what is lost when it runs out; it promises no start
    date. The wording of that line is for the sunset lane's brief.

    and the lane then reports that tool before its rows are read (ROW-2); such a step becomes the
    owner's only by his word (A6). How many draft rows meet this is not established: the author did not
    count them, and nobody has tested a tool.
  - **The id a create returns is no longer written in the journal.** It goes to a protected record as a
    held value and the journal refers to it (LOG-2, EV-1, Example 3).
  - **A command that returns a secret runs only inside an operation** (said to him with A17; the author
    kept it, because the receipt needs a journal and a look has none before the go). What a lane loses,

    comes from. Where the repository holds no env file with it, the frame as handed has no way for the
    look to fetch it. That is a change to what v1 does, and it is put to the owner as one question; not
    ruled here.

  **The risk record (the catalog's Part 6), changed with this entry.** The entry for his word on a lost

  provider repeats, like any other value from a provider, so RK6 as the frame words it (SEC-12) covers

  **The author's own slip, by its report.** One long difference was saved by its harness into a folder

  held its own difference of the frame.
  **Seen by the author and not touched** (for the first builder's brief unless the reviewer grades one):

  **The counting rule for the next check, written before it runs.** D8 marked returned again is a fourth
